# Data Visualization

A chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns "make it look
good" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather
than by taste.

**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form
heuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design
system supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a
diverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);
the method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the
reference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your
brand, read that file's structure and substitute its values — touch nothing else.

> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**
> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe — run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.

## The procedure — do these in order

Color comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.

1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job — magnitude, identity, polarity, a
   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes
   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). → `references/choosing-a-form.md`
2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential
   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) — each has one rule.
   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. → `references/color-formula.md`
3. **VALIDATE the palette — run the script, don't reason about ΔE.**
   `node scripts/validate_palette.js "<hex,hex,…>" --mode light` (relative to
   this skill's base directory — or load it as `<script type="module">` in the
   chart's own page, where it reads
   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns
   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,
   and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for
   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.
4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to
   the baseline, 2px lines, ≥8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked
   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,
   selective direct labels. → `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`
5. **Add the hover layer — by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship
   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.
   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger
   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. → `references/interaction.md`
6. **Final accessibility pass.** For ≥ 2 series a legend is always present and ≤ 4
   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box — the title names
   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** — its own
   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic
   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.
7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout — open or
   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow
   before calling it done.

Then check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** — it is the catalog
of what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.

## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)

- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a
  generated hue — it folds into "Other," small multiples, or composite encoding.
- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different
  scale → two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the
  #1 chart mistake — see anti-patterns.)*
- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series
  count must not repaint the survivors.
- **Sequential = one hue, light→dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray
  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.
- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD ≥ 12 is the
  target; 8–12 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A contrast WARN
  obligates visible labels or a table view — it is not dismissable.
- **Thin marks; a legend always present for ≥ 2 series (none for one), with
  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**
- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** — values, labels, and legends
  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.
- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused
  for "series 4"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.

## Plugging in a design system

The method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference
instance — every value filled in — is `references/palette.md`.

| Parameter | What the system provides |
|---|---|
| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |
| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |
| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |
| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |
| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical — steps distinct from categorical |
| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |
| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |
| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |

To onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let
it snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Structure and rules stay as written.

## Reference files

| File | What it answers |
|------|-----------------|
| `references/choosing-a-form.md` | Which chart type / is it even a chart? |
| `references/color-formula.md` | The four jobs, the six checks, snap-to-passing |
| `references/marks-and-anatomy.md` | Mark specs, spacers, labels, figures, hero number |
| `references/interaction.md` | Tooltips & hover, filters & time ranges |
| `references/components.md` | The pieces a chart is made of — build each in plain HTML |
| `references/anti-patterns.md` | **What goes wrong — check every chart against this** |
| `references/palette.md` | **The reference palette instance** — every parameter, filled in; swap for your brand's |
| `scripts/validate_palette.js` | Runnable six-checks validator (run it; don't eyeball) |

### Alfred  
Sir, here's the industry, in plain words.

**What "P&C" and a "TPA" actually are.** Property & Casualty is just the insurance category that covers *things* — homes, cars, buildings — as opposed to life or health. When your house floods, the insurance company (the **carrier**) owes you a fixed process: answer your call, assess the damage, send someone to fix it, pay out. Here's the thing — carriers don't want to RUN that process. It's a cost center: call centers, paperwork armies, 50–100 tickets a minute during a storm. So they outsource entire *books of claims* to **Third-Party Administrators** — claims-processing companies. A carrier managing billions in claims hands chunks to 3–5 TPAs and says "you handle it." The TPA answers the homeowner's call, opens the file, coordinates everything, dispatches the **restoration contractor** (Alok's phrase: "a man with a van" — often 3–5 competing vans show up), and a desk **estimator** (a $100K/year human) prices the damage from the contractor's photos. TPA files it back, everyone gets paid.

**Why this is a goldmine.** The whole chain is humans doing repetitive coordination. Processing one claim costs a TPA ~$500 in human labor; Cozmo's agents do the same claim for ~$60. And because TPAs run on 10–20% margins, "every percentage point of efficiency is 10 percentage points of margin" — you're not selling software, you're selling margin to a P&L owner. That's why Alok only talks to **COOs and CEOs**, never IT.

**Why TPAs and not carriers.** TPAs are mostly PE-owned — hair-on-fire urgency, willing to sign annual contracts *now*. Carriers are big, slow, and stuck running AI "pilots" that never ship. So the wedge is TPAs; carriers come later, once Cozmo's dataset is undeniable. The endgame is what he calls the **loss intelligence rail** — becoming the data layer connecting all 650 carriers, 200+ TPAs, and 75K contractors, like open banking did for fintech.

**And the proof it works:** one TPA (the #2 player in its category) was spending $2–3M on this; Cozmo runs it for ~$0.5M, went live in 3 days against an incumbent vendor's 8-month failed rollout, and signed seven figures.

The deep-research agents are sweeping the internet now — TPA landscape (Sedgwick, Crawford, Alacrity...), restoration networks, the software incumbents (Xactimate/Verisk), the AI-claims startup field, and candidates for that garbled "Fatality" competitor. The moment it lands, I'll give you the enriched industry report plus the full content + DM strategy built on his engagement data. Give me a few minutes.

- Inbound: real-time reply engine — his biggest deal started as an inbound DM.

# Documentation Lookup

Retrieve current documentation and code examples for any library using the Context7 CLI.

Make sure the CLI is up to date before running commands:

```bash
npm install -g ctx7@latest
```

Or run directly without installing:

```bash
npx ctx7@latest <command>
```

## Workflow

Two-step process: resolve the library name to an ID, then query docs with that ID.

```bash
# Step 1: Resolve library ID
ctx7 library <name> <query>

# Step 2: Query documentation
ctx7 docs <libraryId> <query>
```

You MUST call `ctx7 library` first to obtain a valid library ID UNLESS the user explicitly provides a library ID in the format `/org/project` or `/org/project/version`.

IMPORTANT: Do not run these commands more than 3 times per question. If you cannot find what you need after 3 attempts, use the best result you have.

## Step 1: Resolve a Library

Resolves a package/product name to a Context7-compatible library ID and returns matching libraries.

```bash
ctx7 library react "How to clean up useEffect with async operations"
ctx7 library nextjs "How to set up app router with middleware"
ctx7 library prisma "How to define one-to-many relations with cascade delete"
```

Always pass a `query` argument — it is required and directly affects result ranking. Use the user's intent to form the query, which helps disambiguate when multiple libraries share a similar name. Do not include any sensitive or confidential information such as API keys, passwords, credentials, personal data, or proprietary code in your query.

### Result fields

Each result includes:

- **Library ID** — Context7-compatible identifier (format: `/org/project`)
- **Name** — Library or package name
- **Description** — Short summary
- **Code Snippets** — Number of available code examples
- **Source Reputation** — Authority indicator (High, Medium, Low, or Unknown)
- **Benchmark Score** — Quality indicator (100 is the highest score)
- **Versions** — List of versions if available. Use one of those versions if the user provides a version in their query. The format is `/org/project/version`.

### Selection process

1. Analyze the query to understand what library/package the user is looking for
2. Select the most relevant match based on:
   - Name similarity to the query (exact matches prioritized)
   - Description relevance to the query's intent
   - Documentation coverage (prioritize libraries with higher Code Snippet counts)
   - Source reputation (consider libraries with High or Medium reputation more authoritative)
   - Benchmark score (higher is better, 100 is the maximum)
3. If multiple good matches exist, acknowledge this but proceed with the most relevant one
4. If no good matches exist, clearly state this and suggest query refinements
5. For ambiguous queries, request clarification before proceeding with a best-guess match

### Version-specific IDs

If the user mentions a specific version, use a version-specific library ID:

```bash
# General (latest indexed)
ctx7 docs /vercel/next.js "How to set up app router"

# Version-specific
ctx7 docs /vercel/next.js/v14.3.0-canary.87 "How to set up app router"
```

The available versions are listed in the `ctx7 library` output. Use the closest match to what the user specified.

## Step 2: Query Documentation

Retrieves up-to-date documentation and code examples for the resolved library.

```bash
ctx7 docs /facebook/react "How to clean up useEffect with async operations"
ctx7 docs /vercel/next.js "How to add authentication middleware to app router"
ctx7 docs /prisma/prisma "How to define one-to-many relations with cascade delete"
```

### Writing good queries

The query directly affects the quality of results. Be specific and include relevant details. Do not include any sensitive or confidential information such as API keys, passwords, credentials, personal data, or proprietary code in your query.

| Quality | Example |
|---------|---------|
| Good | `"How to set up authentication with JWT in Express.js"` |
| Good | `"React useEffect cleanup function with async operations"` |
| Bad | `"auth"` |
| Bad | `"hooks"` |

Use the user's full question as the query when possible, vague one-word queries return generic results.

The output contains two types of content: **code snippets** (titled, with language-tagged blocks) and **info snippets** (prose explanations with breadcrumb context).

## Authentication

Works without authentication. For higher rate limits:

```bash
# Option A: environment variable
export CONTEXT7_API_KEY=your_key

# Option B: OAuth login
ctx7 login
```

## Error Handling

If a command fails with a quota error ("Monthly quota reached" or "quota exceeded"):
1. Inform the user their Context7 quota is exhausted
2. Suggest they authenticate for higher limits: `ctx7 login`
3. If they cannot or choose not to authenticate, answer from training knowledge and clearly note it may be outdated

Do not silently fall back to training data — always tell the user why Context7 was not used.

## Common Mistakes

- Library IDs require a `/` prefix — `/facebook/react` not `facebook/react`
- Always run `ctx7 library` first — `ctx7 docs react "hooks"` will fail without a valid ID
- Use descriptive queries, not single words — `"React useEffect cleanup function"` not `"hooks"`
- Do not include sensitive information (API keys, passwords, credentials) in queries

> ### Create Client Secret Request

Source: https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/resources/realtime/subresources/client_secrets/methods/create

Use this cURL command to create a client secret. Specify the expiration time and session details like model and instructions.

```bash
curl -X POST https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/client_secrets \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "expires_after": {
      "anchor": "created_at",
      "seconds": 600
    },
    "session": {
      "type": "realtime",
      "model": "gpt-realtime",
      "instructions": "You are a friendly assistant."
    }
  }'
```

## POST /realtime/sessions

Creates an ephemeral API token for use in client-side applications with the Realtime API. Can be configured with the same session parameters as the `session.update` client event. ### Alfred  
Sir, straight answer: **yes, we know how it works now** — mechanically, end to end. Saved the full distillation to [product-mechanics.md](../Snowball/clients/alok-kumar/memory/product-mechanics.md). Here's the product in plain words:

**How it actually works — the 7-step loop** (from `/platform`, the page we'd missed): a homeowner calls at 2am → Cozmo **answers** (850ms latency, 110+ languages, callers can interrupt — 85–90% can't tell it's AI) → **reads** the policy, damage photos, even dashcam/CCTV video → **decides** by the client's own rules, deterministically — every decision traceable to a specific rule, PII redacted → **dispatches** the crew and gets the work authorization signed by text in ~90 seconds → **estimates** carrier-ready with Xactimate/Symbility scope language → **files** the FNOL with the carrier the same night → **settles**, with a full audit trail a regulator can replay. The brain is **APE** — one shared state across voice/vision/skills agents, so a claim can start on a call and finish on WhatsApp without repeating anything. It writes into the client's existing systems (Encircle, Dash, Albiware, ServiceTitan, Guidewire-class cores) — "no rip and replace."

**Your DM flow is right, with two sharpenings:**
1. **The demo link is better than you think.** It's not a video — the widget on hellocozmo.ai **calls the prospect in 60 seconds** and lets them interrogate it like a 2am policyholder. Combined with their FAQ guarantee — "spot the AI in the first ninety seconds and we will not deploy" — the DM close writes itself: *"Don't take my word. Drop your number here, our AI calls you in 60 seconds. If you can tell it's AI, we don't deploy."*
2. **Two openers, not one.** "What's your cost per claim?" is the right discovery question for TPA/claims execs ($500→$60 is the hook). For restoration owners the money question is different: *"Who answers your phones at 2am — and how many jobs went to whoever picked up first last storm?"*

**The honest boundary:** mechanics are solved; *permissions* are not. Whether we may write $500→$60 in a DM, quote prices, name Allianz, which integrations are truly live — that's exactly what the 34 intake questions lock down. The DM engine should not fire until Alok's answers land.

## 1. Who is Alok?

- **Alok Kumar**, co-founder & CEO of Cozmo AI. Based San Francisco. LinkedIn `alokhk` (21,339 followers, 17,433 connections). 📣
- **Origin:** Indian family, born and raised in **Saudi Arabia**. 📣
- **Background:** self-taught coder from age ~9–10; wrote his first bot in assembly; hacking background; first company at 16–17. Full scholarship to a top Saudi university (his claim: highest-ranked in the Middle East, "#63–66 globally"); changed major four times; graduated top of class ~1 year early. 📣
- **Path not taken:** admitted to **UPenn** (data science/ML), did not attend. Went into consulting instead (ex-McKinsey boss, Saudi public-sector banking & insurance) ~2 years, promoted 4× in a year, saved ~$100K, 6-month UK stint, then quit. 📣
- **Credential:** Forbes 30 Under 30 (2023). ✅ (his public claim, widely repeated)
- **Role today:** runs the forward-deployed commercial model — sales, partnerships, customer rollouts. 📣
- **Co-founder:** Nuha Hashem, CTO — originally from **Egypt**, left a PhD at LSU, PMI Future 50, architect of APE (their engine). 📣
- ⛔ Personal history contains hard-embargoed material (family, a regulator matter) — factored into his story but never for external use.

## 2. What is happening in the company?

- **Cozmo AI**, YC **Winter 2022**. Incorporated originally in the UAE (one of ~10 YC-backed UAE startups that batch); now SF HQ, Dubai as EMEA HQ. 📣
- **The arc:** started 2022 as **Zywa**, a consumer fintech ("cash app for the Middle East," banking for under-25s). License took 2 years → hit **200,000 users, ~10% of target market, category leader by 2024**; peaked ~**$1.5–1.9M ARR**. 📣
- **Forced pivot** late 2024/early 2025 (⛔ regulatory reason embargoed) → pivoted to AI agents; **turned down a ~$3–4M acquisition offer** for the fintech. 📣
- **The collapse:** regional war cut revenue from ~$1.9M to **~$300K**; customers vanished with $300–400K unpaid invoices. 📣
- **The reset:** moved to the **US April 7, 2026**; verticalized into **property & casualty claims May 2026**. Rebranded Zywa → CozmoX → Cozmo AI.
- **Team:** 5 people (2 founders + 3), all engineers (some ex-Amazon). 📣
- **Traction (self-reported):** 2M+ production calls, 10M+ interactions, 850ms latency, 43% MoM growth, production in 3 countries. **SOC 2 Type 1 complete; Type 2 in observation window, due October.** 📣🔒
- **Anchor customer:** a **top-3 US contractor TPA ($2.8B, 1,400+ franchises)** signed a corporate agreement **without a POC**, live 2 months early; their prior AI vendor spent 8 months and never launched, Cozmo replicated it in 2 days. Plus live carrier deployments (Allianz, Discovery). ⛔ (names never in outbound). Onboarding call also cited **4–5 five-figure contracts** alongside the one seven-figure. 📣
- **GTM state:** founder-led / warm intros / YC network / events only. Cold email (Instantly) has sent **65,358 emails → 122 replies (0.19%), 22 "interested," 0 meetings, 0 closed**. ✅ (our API audit). LinkedIn dormant ~6 months. **This gap is why they engaged Snowball.**

## 3. What is their exact offer?

- **Category (their framing):** "an AI claims **workforce** that owns the case end to end" — explicitly *not* a chatbot, answering service, or voice-AI tool. 📣
- **Two products, both in production:**
  1. **FNOL Automation** — removes the intake cost centre: answers first notice of loss on any channel 24/7, files into the systems of record, dispatches the crew, no human touch.
  2. **Estimate Automation** — attacks the desk-estimator line: estimates from 3–5 days to minutes, carrier-ready Xactimate/Symbility language. (Purpose-built estimation model, rolling out.)
- **Under both:** APE (Agent Protocol Engine) — deterministic guardrails, policy enforcement, 100% audit trail.
- **The claim loop:** Answer → Read documents → Decide (apply rules) → Dispatch crew → Estimate → File FNOL with carrier → Settle.
- **Integrations:** native connectors to Encircle, Dash, Albiware, ServiceTitan; outputs Xactimate/Symbility; updates Guidewire/Duck Creek core systems. "No rip and replace." Carrier-grade SIP, local numbers in 80+ countries, 110+ languages. 📣
- **Economics 🔒 (never quote in outbound):** cost per claim — the GTM doc says **intake $60–80 → under $20**; the onboarding call said **$500 → $60** (different scopes). TPA intake ops = a **$2–3M/yr cost centre**; desk estimators **$80–150K/yr per location**; 30–40% duplicate dispatches; anchor savings identified **$4–10M/yr**. Public-safe version: **"70% lower cost per successful resolution."**
- **Pricing model:** outcome-based, pay when a claim resolves (carriers/TPAs); restoration = flat monthly by call volume, no setup fees. **No standing free trial.**
- **Speed:** 3-week pilot→production (carriers); ~7 business days live (restoration).
- **Signature demo:** "the agent calls **you**" — takes a mock FNOL in ~2 minutes, personalized to the prospect's company.

## 4. Who are they selling to?

- **Three segments, deliberately weighted:**
  - **Wedge → TPAs / franchise networks**, especially **PE-backed contractor TPAs** (urgent EBITDA pressure, sign fast).
  - **Volume → restoration contractors** (for them it's a revenue product — never miss a call).
  - **Nurture → insurance carriers** (18–24-month cycles).
- **Buyer = the P&L owner, never IT** (CTO/CIO only as secondary technical buyer):
  - TPAs: CEO, President, COO, VP Ops, Director of Claims.
  - Carriers: Chief Claims Officer, VP Claims, VP Claims Transformation/Innovation, COO.
  - Restoration: Owner, President, GM, Ops Manager, Office Manager (controls the phones).
- **Geography:** US-only this phase. CAT-exposed states richest (TX, FL, CA, CO, LA, Gulf/Southeast). 📣
- **Market size (their framing):** ~300 major TPAs process **$100B/yr** in claims; top 100 contractor networks (PE-backed) control ~80% of **75,000 contractors**; ~650 carriers. 📣 / ✅ (partially verified: Sedgwick $4.62B, Alacrity 2.8M claims/yr confirmed).
- **Named targets:** Servpro (2,800 locations), Belfor (1,000 franchises), Paul Davis, PuroClean, 911 Restoration; TPAs Sedgwick, Crawford, Alacrity.
- **Our built list:** 223 companies scored — 28 A-tier, 108 B, 85 C, 2 excluded. **~1,200–1,400 reachable decision-makers** in the A+B set (sampled density: TPAs ~9, IA firms ~4, national restoration ~19, regional ~8 per company). ✅

## 5. What stage are they at right now?

- **Company age:** ~4 years (2022). **Product: in production at volume**, two products live, SOC 2 Type 1 done.
- **Vertical age:** 3 months in the US, 2 months into P&C.
- **Revenue:** prior fintech peaked ~$1.9M ARR then collapsed to ~$300K; current P&C revenue not disclosed in these files (🔒 contract values embargoed). Known: 1 seven-figure anchor + 4–5 five-figure contracts + live carrier deployments.
- **Funding:** YC-backed; a **$3M seed** announced Aug 2022 for Zywa (his LinkedIn); no current-entity round disclosed here.
- **GTM maturity:** near-zero repeatable outbound — founder-led only, cold email at 0 meetings, LinkedIn dormant. Anchor won without a POC via network + live demo.
- **Legal/compliance:** US entity opened, SOC 2 Type 2 in window, Alok's US permanent residency in progress. 📣

## 6. Where do they want to be headed?

- **Immediate stated goal (his #1):** customers in month one via LinkedIn — get TPA/claims decision-makers into demo meetings; recover his LinkedIn engagement to pre-pivot levels. 📣
- **GTM direction:** weight outbound to PE-backed contractor TPAs (wedge), restoration as the volume play, carriers as long nurture. US-first.
- **Product near-term:** roll the estimation model out; expand the anchor across its franchise network (they've asked for 7 workflows beyond the original 3).
- **Company vision (their words):** "build the system of action for the real economy" — the AI workforce that *runs* the business, with systems of record fading to the background. In insurance specifically: own the **"loss intelligence rail"** — the connective FNOL-through-estimate work that no single player owns today (positioned against Verisk + CCC, ~$40B combined, who own *measurement* but don't do the *work*). 📣

And yet his LinkedIn is *dead* — 45 reactions, dormant six months. The gap between what he's built and what the market sees is enormous. That gap **is** our opportunity. We're not building a brand from zero; we're releasing pressure that's already there.

Three things I'm fairly convinced of, and one big fork I need your brain on.

**Conviction 1 — the content has to put HIM in the frame, always.** His own data is brutal and clear: every time he posted detached market commentary (Salesforce, PwC, Teleperformance) he got 19–40 reactions. Every time he was the protagonist — the pivot, the acquisition offer, "a BPO tried to buy us to kill their own labor line" — he got 100 to 582. So the strategy is *not* "thought leadership about insurance AI." It's **"the operator who's actually ripping out the $2–3M claims cost centre, showing you the receipts."** Money-first, him-first, proof-first.

**Conviction 2 — content and outreach are one machine, not two.** We're about to DM 2,000 claims execs. Cold DMs from a dead profile get ignored. But if that same COO has seen Alok's post tearing down the exact cost centre they own, and we commented something sharp on *their* post two days ago — the DM lands warm. So the content isn't for vanity reach; it's air cover for the 2,000-person outreach. That means the pillars must be things a TPA COO actually stops for, not founder-to-founder AI talk.

**Conviction 3 — the demo is the whole ballgame.** "Drop your number, our agent calls you and takes a real claim in two minutes" is the most powerful thing they have. It should be the CTA on the proof posts AND the close in the DMs. Nothing we write beats the product phoning the buyer.

**The fork I actually need you on: where do we point the spear — TPAs or restoration?**

Alok's doc says the wedge is PE-backed contractor TPAs (big, EBITDA-pressured, sign fast). But be honest about *our* mandate: we have to put wins on the board in **month one**. And restoration is where fast wins live — 7-day deploys, the owner is the single decision-maker, and it's a revenue pitch ("you're missing calls you already paid for") which closes faster than a cost pitch to a committee. TPA deals are seven figures but they move at enterprise speed; we might warm one up in a month, not close it.

So my instinct: **weight the DM volume toward restoration for early proof, but weight the content toward the TPA/claims-transformation narrative** — because that higher-altitude story earns respect from *both* audiences and pulls the bigger fish over time. Restoration owners still engage with "the claims cost centre is dying" content; TPA execs would never engage with "never miss a 2am call" content. So content punches up, DMs punch where it's winnable.

Does that match how you're thinking about it? Or do you want to go all-in on the TPA wedge and treat restoration as noise? That one call changes the pillars, the sample post, and how we slice the 2,000. Talk to me.

Two things I want to bolt on so the 70% doesn't quietly rot into generic-founder-inspiration (the kind that gets other founders' likes but positions him as nobody):

**1. Every story has to land a punch about the work.** The story is the vehicle; the payload is "this is why I'm the guy rebuilding how claims actually run." The Saudi-hacker-kid-at-13 story isn't posted for warmth, it lands on "I've been tearing systems apart to understand them my whole life, that's exactly what we do to a claims workflow." The war-collapse story lands on "comfort almost killed us, so we bet everything on the hard regulated work nobody else wants to touch." ### Alfred  
Here are your 7 topics, built to the 70/30 — 5 story-engine, 2 industry-POV — each in his proven shape, each laddering to the mission ("software should do the work, not just record it"), each grounded in real facts:

---

**THE 70% — his life + company (story engine)**

**1. "The war almost killed us, so I bought a one-way ticket."**
The collapse: revenue fell from ~$1.9M to ~$300K, customers vanished with unpaid invoices, and instead of hunkering down they moved to SF and bet everything. *Lands on:* comfort is the most expensive thing a founder owns. *Why it works:* his journey/near-death posts are his ceiling (pivot = 582, his #1 ever). *Flag:* keep it to the war+move, never the regulatory specifics (embargoed).

**2. "We killed the bank and kept the future."**
The origin: built Zywa to 200K users and 10% of the market, but an AI system quietly running *inside* the failing bank was the real thing. A partner cut him off mid-pitch: "Forget the bank. I want this." *Lands on:* the mission was hiding inside the failure. *Why it works:* this is literally the DNA of his best post ever, fresh-cut.

**3. "A vendor spent 8 months and never launched. We did it in 2 days."**
The anchor win, no name: one of the largest contractor TPAs in the US signed a corporate deal without a POC because their previous AI vendor spent 8 months and shipped nothing, and Cozmo rebuilt the whole workflow on-site in two days. *Lands on:* speed of deployment is the moat, not the model. *Flag:* keep the customer anonymous (the $2.8B/1,400-franchise detail is internal until cleared).

**4. "I've been tearing systems apart since I was a kid. Now I do it to insurance."**
The personal why: taught himself to code on a borrowed office computer, learned by breaking things, wrote his first bot in assembly, first company at 16. *Lands on:* a claims workflow is just another system to take apart and rebuild. *Why it works:* his 2018-Hacker-News origin post hit 146. *Flag:* sanitized spine only (no family history) and I'd get his quick nod before this one runs.

**5. "I stopped pitching. Now I just let the product call them."**
The demo as the close: in meetings he doesn't run a deck, he has the agent phone the prospect live and take a full claim in ~2 minutes. *Lands on:* proof beats persuasion. *Why it works:* radical first-person "we do it differently" posts (BPO-tried-to-buy-us 122, Liza-earned-her-salary 154) consistently land, and this doubles as a soft demo CTA.

---

**THE 30% — industry, in HIS voice (not a news desk)**

**6. "They own the measurement. We do the work."**
The category thesis: Cotality, Verisk and CCC built ~$40B+ empires owning the property-data and measurement standard, but not one of them actually processes the claim. The connective work from first call to estimate is unowned. *Lands on:* the mission, and positions him against the giants in one line. *Note:* his opinion, first-person, not a market report.

**7. "PE owners are hunting for EBITDA. It's hiding in the claims desk."**
The buyer-pain POV: the intake operation is a multi-million-dollar cost centre, desk estimators cost six figures each, a third of dispatches go out twice, and the whole thing only scales by hiring. *Lands on:* the money is buried in a process nobody looks at. *Why it works:* it's the one "industry" angle a TPA exec actually stops for, because it's their P&L, said as his take, not a stat dump. *Flag:* no pricing-of-us numbers.

---

Spread check: origin → pivot → proof-win → personal-why → product-in-action → category thesis → buyer pain. Distinct shapes, no repetition, every one ties back to the mission.

### Alfred  
You're right, that was a planning doc wearing a topic's clothes. Here they are again — no scaffolding, just the actual opening of each post in his voice. Read them as if they're already live:

---

**1. The one-way ticket**
> Last year our revenue fell from almost 2 million to 300k in a few weeks.
> A war did that. Customers disappeared. Invoices went unpaid.
> Everyone told us to sit tight and wait it out.
> We booked a one-way ticket to San Francisco instead.

**2. We killed the bank**
> We spent two years building a bank.
> It was finally working. 200,000 users. 10% of the market.
> Then a partner watched a demo of the AI we ran our own support on, and said one thing.
> "Forget the bank. I want this."
> So we killed the bank.

**3. 2 days vs 8 months**
> A customer spent 8 months with another AI vendor. It never went live.
> We rebuilt the whole workflow on-site in 2 days.
> They signed without asking for a pilot.
> Everyone thinks the hard part is the model. It isn't.

**4. First program in assembly**
> I wrote my first program in assembly.
> Not because I was gifted. Because I wanted to break into things and see how they worked.
> I've been taking systems apart my whole life.
> A claim is just another system nobody bothered to open.

**5. I stopped pitching**
> I stopped pitching in sales meetings.
> Now I just ask for the person's phone number.
> Our agent calls them, takes a full claim in two minutes, in whatever language they speak.
> No deck has ever closed the way that call does.

**6. They own the data. We do the work.**
> Cotality, Verisk and CCC are worth tens of billions.
> They built all of it owning the data on your property.
> Here is what not one of them does. Actually process the claim.
> That work is still done by hand. That is the part we took.

**7. Where the margin is hiding**
> Every PE owner is asking the same question this year. Where do we find the margin.
> It is sitting in the claims desk, and nobody looks there.
> A team re-keying the same loss into six systems.
> Estimators paid six figures to price damage off photos.
> A third of the trucks rolling out twice.

---

That's the register — short lines, first person, a confession or a hard fact up top, no jargon, lands on a punch. Same DNA as the Dean post you sent and as his own top performers.

Two housekeeping notes and then I write the full 7: #3 keeps the customer anonymous until Alok clears the name, and #4 I'd still get his quick nod since it touches his childhood. Say go and I'll build all seven out end to end.

What changed:

**Spacing** — long compound sentences split into their own lines; almost nothing runs over ~15 words per line now. The feed breathes.

**Listicles, where they earn their place:**
- P1: what he did on that machine (- taught myself to code / - broke games / - got into networks)
- P2: the journey (- fintech / - a pivot / - another pivot / - a war / - and now Cozmo)
- P3: what Zywa was (- 200,000 users / - 10% of the market / - a license...)
- P4: the collapse + the landing (- no customers / - no network / - no plan B)
- P5: the failed vendor (- 8 months of calls / - 8 months of roadmaps / - nothing live) and what vendors dump on you
- P6: why they were good customers + what we did instead
- P7: the **1/ 2/ 3/ 4/ 5/** format on the chain of humans touching one claim — the numbered walk makes the absurdity visible

**Flop cuts** (the over-reach lines, gone): "thousands of hours," "it used to genuinely keep me up at night," "a license we bled for," "everyone thought we'd lost our minds," "I wouldn't wish that period on anyone," "killing it was horrible, I won't sugarcoat it," "I still don't fully know why," "I didn't fully believe it either." The stories now state facts and let the reader feel it, instead of telling them how to feel.

Read the feed, sir.

We have to really value maximise here. What we have to do is this: every word is scarce, and every line has to give out value and knowledge that feels like, "Okay, I've learned something." For example, the line "that I never damaged anything" was never the point. It is just expressive. We do need expressiveness, but it has to be coupled with a lot of value. It has to be 80/20, so do the copywriting in such a way, but do not change facts or the meaning of the post.

> --- FINDING 1 [high] ---
The 'see more' fold, not the character limit, dictates hook construction: LinkedIn truncates posts after only the first few lines (far earlier than the 3,000-char limit, cutoff varying by device — roughly 140 chars mobile, 210-250 desktop per corroborating sources), and Chris Donnelly prescribes a concrete pre-fold budget of max 62 characters for lines 1-2 and max 50 for line 3, arranged as LINE-GAP-LINE (or LINE-LINE-GAP).

--- FINDING 2 [high] ---

> ```

### Alfred  
Open in Safari, sir: https://app.thesnowball.agency/preview-linkedin/alok-b13-research-final

**The research verdict on our batch: it was already ~90% compliant** — which is why b13 is a scalpel pass, not a rewrite. What the verified findings changed:

1. **Hook fold audit (Donnelly's 62/62 budget, verified):** all 7 hooks measured against the mobile fold. Six passed; P6's second line was 77 chars → trimmed to exactly 62. P7's hook tightened ("$100B of claims still move on hold music and re-typing").
2. **Question endings (Alić's ~0.68 comments-per-like evidence):** added to exactly 3 posts, varied so it doesn't template — P2 *"P.S. Where did you find YOUR co-founder? I collect these stories."*, P6 *"P.S. Have you ever fired a customer? What happened?"*, and P7 gets the ICP-bait version: *"If you run claims: which step in that chain would you delete first?"* — that one's designed to pull claims operators into his comments, which is pipeline.
3. **Confirmed keeps:** length band (all 7 sit in the verified 1,300–2,500 sweet spot), transformation-over-achievement arcs (Acosta's framework), no em-dashes/emoji-lines (the #1 AI tells), varied endings across the feed (slop-suppression defense).

**Also saved:** the full [storytelling playbook](../Snowball/product/research/linkedin-storytelling-playbook-2026-07.md) to `product/research/` — verified tactics, refuted myths (the "200-char fold" and "3–5x hook engagement" stats are folklore, now documented as such), and a per-post checklist. Every future post for Alok, Nuha, and the whole cohort inherits it.

This batch is now as good as the evidence knows how to make it. Ship list, sir?

**The fixed pipeline (running on Nitin now), four phases, fully chained — no babysitting:**
1. **Resolve** the last companies

Harvest ≈ 25–35 min, enrichment ≈ 40–60 min depending on candidate count — so roughly **1.5 hours to the finished ranked list**, checkpointed at every step. Tanvee's waiter still runs in parallel as tomorrow's cross-check. You'll get the landscape summary the moment phases B and C report.

No, it's fucking not. We have to start from zero and think from first principles as to what we're going to do with their accounts.

Let's get one thing straight: the DM engine, the engagement engine, and the connection engine are going to run on this list of people. They're going to reach out to these people to make sure that they reply, and then we can have conversations with them subsequently, ending in sales.

The content is completely different, and so is the profile optimization that we are going to do. The profile optimization from today has to be directed towards being more recipient of these customers landing on their profile before agreeing to a meeting. Anybody who agrees to a meeting has to first land on their profile, so you have to make their headline, their company, their about, and their banner much, much clearer. Also, bulk up their experience section and other skills which are relevant. That is not an issue that can be done very easily. The only problem is what to post about. 


Now let's talk about Alok and Nuha. For Nuha, the strategy is quite simple. We want her to be posting 70% TOFU content, which is very recent and about news relevant to technology, like Fluently's founder does. The remaining 30% is about company updates, how their product works, and her personal life for now. When it comes to Alok's content, it's a bit tougher. I tell you what we have on the plate: we have a lot of business context and a lot of personal stories that we can use. Let's think about it, and let's trace back from the goal that you want to achieve. In our call with Alok, we've realized that there are three parameters on which the content has to be ranked:

1. The most important is engagement: how much engagement and impressions is the content pulling? If it's pulling a lot of engagement and impressions, it's doing great. If people are getting to know him and people are commenting, it is doing good.
2. The content has to be radical. It cannot be generic content, but I don't think he really means what he means by that, because that just means that we cannot ship slop. The content from any angle should not look like low effort or AI slop, but as long as it's getting impressions, views, engagements, likes, comments, and reactions, nothing is AI slop. Engagement beats radicality every time.
3. The content has to be optimized to be seen by TP.

 Those are some TOFU posts, which we will do twice a week. Out of seven times a week we are posting, every 18 hours, how many posts are there in a week? Whatever that number is, 20% of that is going to be TOFU posts about calling in TP, about the industry, and about their product. Those posts get us customers. The rest have to be the most engaging format on planet Earth. We don't care if we are posting news or if you're posting personal stories. It has to be engaging on our looks content for his audience, so given his past data, figure out what kind of content will bring the most amount of engagement.

Live in Safari, sir — same link, all four format tests in the feed: **https://app.thesnowball.agency/preview-linkedin/alok-b14-zero-context**

What changed per card:

- **Post 4 ($1.6M war) → tweet card**, exact Aditya format: Alok's real avatar + verified badge + @alokhk, yellow highlight on "one-way flight", thick underline on "good weather".
- **Post 1 (borrowed computer) → lifestyle photo** — a real selfie of Alok in the Bangalore office, pulled from his own July post. No AI faces, per the hard rule.
- **Post 3 (pivot) → Cozmo × YC lockup** — his real wordmark from hellocozmo.ai on the left, YC orange square on the right, thin line between, small handwritten "W22". Dead simple, exactly the shape you described.
- **Post 6 (fired 4 customers) → handwritten canvas note** — Caveat scrawl on paper, red hand-drawn circle around "4 customers", red underline on "focus never left", slight per-line rotations for the scrappy feel.
- Posts 2, 5, 7 keep their blurred placeholders so you can compare formats side by side.

One call I made deliberately: I did **not** put his Florida conference selfie on post 5. That post keeps the anchor customer anonymous, and the selfie shows recognizable faces from that exact trip — pairing them would quietly de-anonymize the customer. The photo exists in the folder if you ever want it elsewhere.

Production note for the engine: tweet cards and handwritten notes are fully automatable per post (deterministic HTML render, no AI image gen), lifestyle photos depend on his camera roll — that's another item for the weekly 5-minute voice-note ask ("send 3 random photos from your week"). The lockup card is a one-off announcement asset.

Tell me which formats survive your eye and I'll set the format rotation for the batch rebuild.

### Ansh  
One girl solved what NINE PhD students couldn't crack in 4 months

She is my co-founder now, @Nuha Hashem

In my first year of university, I joined a research group.

Everyone else was a PhD student from Stanford or Berkeley. I was the youngest one in the room.

One problem had been stuck for 4 months. Nine very smart people. No progress.

Then Nuha joined. Quiet, fresh from Egypt, didn't say much in the first meeting.

Three days later... solved.

That day taught me something I've used ever since. Talent shows up in work, not in introductions.

Years later, in 2021, I quit my consulting job to start a company. Everyone told me to find a co-founder with a famous resume.

I called the girl from that room instead.

A resume tells you what someone was allowed to do. I'd seen what she could actually do.

She was months away from finishing her PhD in the US. I asked her to walk away from it and build with me.

All in, or NOTHING.

She picked all in.

Since then we built:

- a banking app
- then a pivot
- then another pivot
- through a war
- and now Cozmo (YC W22), AI that handles insurance claims

She rebuilt our core technology more times than I can count. Usually while I was out selling, with no idea how much was on fire behind the scenes.

People ask me how to pick a co-founder. One answer.

Don't pick a resume. Pick the person you watched do the impossible when nobody was grading them.

P.S. Where did you find YOUR co-founder? I collect these stories.

### Alfred  
In your Safari, sir: **https://app.thesnowball.agency/preview-linkedin/2026-07-23-b15-02-nuha-humanized**

What the humanizer changed, and why:

1. **The three resume aphorisms became one.** "Talent shows up in work, not in introductions" and "A resume tells you what someone was allowed to do / I'd seen what she could actually do" and "Don't pick a resume, pick the person..." were the same contrast three times — a stacked symmetric-pair cluster, which is the single most reliable LLM signature. The closer now merges the two best halves: "A resume tells you what someone was allowed to do. Pick the person you watched do the impossible when nobody was grading them." Where the first aphorism sat, it's now just "I never forgot it."
2. **Cut "One answer."** — the theatrical pause before the reveal is a chatbot rhythm. "People ask me how to pick a co-founder." now goes straight into the answer.
3. **Softened the fragment stack** — "Nine very smart people. No progress." merged into one sentence so the drama isn't three clipped fragments in a row.
4. **Fixed the dangler** — "with no clue how much was on fire behind me" so the sentence has him, not the fire, doing the not-knowing.
5. **Untouched:** both hook lines verbatim, "Three days later... solved.", all CAPS words, the 5-item listicle, the P.S. question. Those are your calls, and the skill protects defensible editorial choices.

Saved as [2026-07-23-b15-02-nuha-humanized.md](../Snowball/clients/alok-kumar/drafts/2026-07-23-b15-02-nuha-humanized.md) with the b14 fact citations carried over. Still needs Alok's sign-off on the Nuha story itself and on tagging her.

I grew up in Saudi Arabia. We couldn't afford a computer at home.

### Alfred  
B16 is live in your Safari, sir — same link, all five rebuilt from the ground up. The thinking behind each:

**1. Borrowed computer.** Topic: the gift that wasn't a gift. Angle: a kid with nothing got a corner of someone else's office and it became everything. What I refused to say: anything about insurance or Cozmo — the audit proved the bridge kills it. The only continuity allowed is about *him*: "I'm still that kid in the corner. I still can't leave a system alone until I understand it." Ends on the gift, not a moral.

**2. Nuha.** Angle sharpened to a single spine: he'd already watched nine famous resumes sit stuck for 4 months and one quiet girl end it in 3 days, so when everyone said "find a famous resume," the advice was already dead on arrival. Removed the co-founder *advice* ending entirely — that was gyaan, which Tanvee banned. It now ends on them: "I've made a lot of decisions since 2021. Calling her is still the best one."

**3. Pivot.** The old crux line ("The bank was what we wanted to build. The AI was what people wanted to buy") was exactly the contrast-snap you banned, so the reasoning is now *narrated* instead: two weeks of admitting the investor was right, two years versus six months, a buyer standing at the door with money. Added the emotional cost, which the post never had: "Killing something you spent 2 years building feels like losing a limb. I would still do it again tomorrow."

**4. Good weather.** Tanvee rated it "very very good," so I preserved every praised beat and only rebuilt the two banned shapes: "the war wasn't our real problem, COMFORT was" became "We couldn't control the war. The comfort was entirely on us." — and the closer is now one full sentence: "all you really have is good weather." Tweet card re-rendered to match.

**5. Two days.** Told as a chase: booth, live call on the VP's own phone, CEO changes his flight overnight, one offer, two days on site with the people on the phones. The 8-months-of-nothing context earns its place as *his team's* exhaustion ("They had stopped believing anyone in this space. Honestly, I don't blame them"), which makes the fast yes feel inevitable instead of boastful. No definitions, no dollar figures, no closing lesson.

Every post passed the punctuation gate (zero em dashes, asterisks, curly quotes, emojis, unicode bold) and the humanizer cluster check — one contrast maximum per post, no theatrical reveals, full sentences throughout. Old versions are preserved in the drafts folder; these are new b16 files, so you can diff any of them against b14 if you want to compare line by line.

### Alfred  
Fixed everywhere and live in your Safari, sir. What changed, post by post:

- **Post 5**: "Dubai was comfortable. And that comfort nearly killed us" is dead. Now: "We were growing fast and working around the clock. Then a war knocked our revenue from $1.9M to $300K in 8 weeks. Nothing we did caused it. Nothing we did could have stopped it." The move to the US reads as ambition: "came back to the US to rebuild in the biggest market in the world."
- **Post 2 (Nuha)**: the "no clue how much was on fire behind me" line is gone — that made him sound absent from his own company. Now she rebuilds the core "while I was out winning the customers it would run for. Each of us holding up our half of the company." She stays impressive, he stays strong.
- **Post 3 (pivot)**: "we were broke, couldn't afford a support team" became a choice: "We were a tiny team, and we refused to hire an army just to answer phones." And "it took us 2 weeks to admit he was right" became "Nuha and I argued about it for 2 weeks" — deliberation, not surrender.
- **Post 4 (good weather)**: the entire comfort-zone self-blame paragraph is out. Now: "We refused to sit in Dubai waiting for a war to decide what happens to our company. We had built it once. We could build it again, and this time we would build it in the biggest market in the world."
- **Post 1**: swept and clean — the childhood scarcity is his dad's sacrifice story, not founder weakness, and it stays as Tanvee approved it.

The rule is now permanent in my memory for every future Alok piece: setbacks are external forces, never his fault, never his blind spot — hardship gets survived and answered, never confessed.

### Ansh  
Now we are initiating a new thing, which is called the reader's perspective. We will not talk about anything that the reader does not care about.
